Z_{V}=\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2N}\mathrm{Tr}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial M^{2}}}\textrm{e}^{NV(M)}\right]_{M=0}=\left[\frac{1}{\Delta(\mathbf{x})}\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2N}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{x}})^{2}}\Delta(\mathbf{x})\,\textrm{e}^{NV(\mathbf{x})}\right]_{\mathbf{x}=0}

(291)

where
(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{x}})^{2}=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{i}^{2}}
.
The formula is clearly well-normalized, since
(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{x}})^{2}\Delta(\mathbf{x})=0
.
Note that the differential reformulation of the usual integral over eigenvalues Eq. (
243
) reads

Z_{V}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}{b_{N}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{N}{2}\mathbf{x}^{2}}\Delta^{2}(\mathbf{x})\,\textrm{e}^{NV(\mathbf{x})}=e_{N}\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2N}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{x}})^{2}}\Delta^{2}(\mathbf{x})\,\textrm{e}^{NV(\mathbf{x})}\right]_{\mathbf{x}=0},

(292)

where the normalization
e_{N}=N^{N(N-1)/2}/\prod_{j=1}^{N}j!
is computed in Appendix
13
.

For the diagonalization of two-matrix models it is crucial that we have a differential formulation using only one matrix such as Eq. (
272
).
It is not possible to simultaneously diagonalize differential operators involving two matrices such as
\mathrm{Tr}\,(\frac{\partial}{\partial A}\frac{\partial}{\partial B})
in Eq. (
269
), at least not by the arguments used here.
However, the differential formulation in terms of a single matrix allows us to diagonalize two-matrix models.

Applying Eq. (
282
) to Eq. (
272
) yields

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=\left[\frac{1}{\Delta(\mathbf{x})}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{x}})}\Delta(\mathbf{x})\,\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathbf{x})}\right]_{\mathbf{x}=0}\,.

(293)

For comparison, the differential formulation of the usual integral over eigenvalues Eq. (
254
) is

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=e_{N}\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial a_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{i}}}\,\Delta(\mathbf{a})\Delta(\mathbf{b})\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\mathbf{a})}\,\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\mathbf{b})}\right]_{\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{b}=0}

(294)

where the normalization is the same as for the one-matrix model (see Appendix
13
).

The equivalence between Eq. (
291
) and Eq. (
292
) on one hand, and between Eq. (
293
) and Eq. (
294
) on the other hand is not manifest in this form, however it becomes clear when formulated in terms of Slater determinants, as detailed later in Sec.
26.1.3
for the two-matrix case and in Sec.
26.3.1
for the one-matrix model.

26
Expressions as Slater determinants and applications

26.1
Equivalent formulations of a two-matrix model

In this first subsection, we detail the steps leading to the Slater determinant formulation of the partition functions of two-matrix models, in differential formulation.
The same holds for the one-matrix models, as the special case
V_{1}(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}
(cf. Sec.
26.3.1
).

26.1.1
Determinant form

Starting from Eq. (
293
):

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=\left[\frac{1}{\Delta(\mathbf{x})}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}})}\right)\Delta(\mathbf{x})\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}x_{i})}\right)\right]_{\mathbf{x}=0},

(295)

we incorporate the products on the left and right of the Vandermonde determinant into a Slater determinant:

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=\left[\frac{1}{\Delta(\mathbf{x})}\det\left\{\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x_{i}})}x_{i}^{j-1}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}x_{i})}\right\}_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\right]_{\mathbf{x}=0}.

(296)

We can extract the symmetric part of the determinant by using the following identity (
[
161
]
, Thm. 1.2.4 p. 24):

\left[\frac{1}{\Delta(\mathbf{x})}\det_{i,j}\bigl(f_{j}(x_{i})\bigr)\right]_{\forall i,\,x_{i}=x}=f_{N}\,\det_{i,j}\left(f_{j}^{(i-1)}(x)\right),

(297)

with
f_{N}=1/\prod_{j=1}^{N-1}j!
.
48
48

48

The factor
(-1)^{\frac{N(N-1)}{2}}
in the reference is due to a different convention in the definition of the Vandermonde determinant.
This leads to the following Slater determinant:

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=f_{N}\det\left\{\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i}}{\mathrm{d}x^{i}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}x)}\right]_{x=0}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1}.

(298)

This is again well-normalized, since for
V_{1}=V_{2}=0
the determinant is
\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}i!
.

26.1.2
Biorthogonal polynomials

As in Sec.
24.2.5
,
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i}}{\mathrm{d}x^{i}}
and
x^{j}
can be replaced in Eq. (
298
) by any monic polynomials
P_{i}(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})
,
Q_{j}(x)
respectively of degrees
i
and
j
:

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=f_{N}\det\left\{\left[P_{i}\Bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\Bigr)\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{d}{dx})}Q_{j}(x)\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}x)}\right]_{x=0}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1},

(299)

the differential formulation of the bilinear form of Eq. (
258
) being:

\langle f|g\rangle=c_{1}\left[f\biggl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\biggr)\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}g\biggl(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}}\biggr)\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}x)}\right]_{x=0}.

(300)

From this, one can apply the method of biorthogonal polynomials.

26.1.3
Equivalence between the diagonalized formulations for two-matrix models

Expanding the Vandermonde determinants in Eq. (
294
) as in Sec.
24.2.4
, we obtain:

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=e_{N}N!\det\left\{\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial a}\frac{\partial}{\partial b}}a^{i}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(a)}b^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(b)}\right]_{a=b=0}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1}.

(301)

This is seen to be equivalent to Eq. (
298
), since, from Eq. (
277
) which also holds when
A,B
are real variables (and seeing
N
as a real coefficient):

\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i}}{\mathrm{d}x^{i}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{d}{dx})}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}x)}\right]_{x=0}=N^{i/2}\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\partial}{\partial a}\frac{\partial}{\partial b}}a^{i}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(a)}b^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}b)}\right]_{a=b=0},

(302)

which in turn by the change of variable
b=\sqrt{N}b^{\prime}
is equal to

N^{\frac{i+j}{2}}[\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial a}\frac{\partial}{\partial b}}a^{i}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(a)}b^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(b)}]_{a=b=0}.

The normalizations do agree since
f_{N}=e_{N}N!/N^{N(N-1)/2}
.

Eq. (
301
) is in turn seen to be equivalent to the Hankel determinant Eq. (
257
), as from Eq. (
269
) for
N=1
:

\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial a}\frac{\partial}{\partial b}}a^{i}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(a)}b^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(b)}\right]_{a=b=0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y}{c_{1}}\,\textrm{e}^{-Nxy}x^{i}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(x)}y^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(y)},

(303)

where we recall that
c_{1}=\frac{2\pi}{iN}
, so that
e_{N}N!/(c_{1})^{N}=N^{\frac{N(N-1)}{2}}f_{N}/(c_{1})^{N}=1/d_{N}
.

26.1.4
Expansion over Schur functions

Performing steps similar to Sec.
24.2.4
but in the differential formulation and the opposite direction, we may re-express Eq. (
298
) as:

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=\frac{f_{N}}{N!}\left[{\Delta\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{x}}\biggr)}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{x}})}\Delta(\mathbf{x})\,\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathbf{x})}\right]_{\mathbf{x}=0}.

(304)

As explained in (
[
162
]
, Eq (2.2.4)), the expression Eq. (
304
) is easily seen — by its action on Schur functions — to be a scalar product on symmetric functions, with

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=f_{N}\left\langle\exp\left(NV_{1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\,\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\,\right)\right),\exp\left(NV_{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\,\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\,\right)\right)\right\rangle.

(305)

Using the orthogonality of Schur functions for this scalar product and the Cauchy-Littlewood formula, one obtains directly an explicit double-series expansion over Schur functions (Eq. (2.2.13) in
[
162
]
), which guarantees the fact that
Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}
is a tau function of the Toda hierarchy.

26.2
Integration over one variable in the determinant form of two-matrix models

In order to verify the equivalence — in differential formulation — between Eq. (
291
) and Eq. (
292
), it is required to go to Slater determinant form, and then transform certain derivatives to variables.
We do this for general
V_{1}
, as the computation is the same in differential formulation for general
V_{1}
or for
V_{1}(x)=x^{2}/2
(corresponding to the one-matrix models).
We also detail the computation in the integral formulation for general
V_{1}
, which is naturally more involved than for
V_{1}(x)=x^{2}/2
, in which case it is a simple Gaussian integration. This leads to a new determinant formulation of the partition functions of two-matrix models, and we comment on its potential use for resolutions involving orthogonal polynomials instead of biorthogonal polynomials.

26.2.1
General computation

We start from Eq. (
298
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z&=f_{N}\det_{i,j}\left\{\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i}}{\mathrm{d}x^{i}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(\frac{x}{\sqrt{N}})}\right]_{x=0}\right\}\\
&=f_{N}\det_{i,j}\left\{\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i}}{\mathrm{d}x^{i}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]_{x=0}\right\},\end{split}

(306)

where
0\leq i,j\leq N-1
, by change of variable.
49
49

49

The factors
\sqrt{N}^{j-i}
from the change of variable compensate when factorized out of the determinant.
In this subsection, we assume
V_{1}
to be a polynomial:

V_{1}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\frac{\alpha_{k}}{k}x^{k},\qquad p\geq 1.

(307)

We write, as in Eq. (
277
) but for real variables:

\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i}}{\mathrm{d}x^{i}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]_{x=0}=\left[\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}}y^{i}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}\right]_{y=0}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]_{x=0}.

(308)

We first notice that:

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}=(N^{1-p}\alpha_{p})^{r}S_{(p-1)r}(y)\,\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(y)},

(309)

where
S_{(p-1)r}(y)
is a monic polynomial of degree
(p-1)r
.

Let us consider the row
i=(p-1)r+s
, where
s\in\{0,1,\ldots,p-1\}
. We can replace in the determinant the right-hand side of Eq. (
308
) by

\Bigl[\Bigl[\textrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}}y^{s}S_{(p-1)r}(y)\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}\Bigr]_{y=0}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\Bigr]_{x=0}

(310)

and therefore by

Z=f_{N}\det_{i,j}\left\{(N^{1-p}\alpha_{p})^{-r(i)}\left[\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}}y^{s(i)}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r(i)}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r(i)}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}\right]_{y=0}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]_{x=0}\right\},

(311)

where
0\leq i,j<N
. We now show that:

\left[\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}}y^{s}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}\right]_{y=0}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]_{x=0}=\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}x^{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\Bigl[x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\Bigr]\right]_{x=0},

(312)

that is, just as for Eq. (
277
) or Eq. (
308
), the result of the inner bracket
[...]_{y=0}
on the left-hand side is obtained by “replacing” the
y
’s by
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}
’s, but also the
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}
’s by
y
’s, while reversing the left-to-right ordering.

Developing first the right hand side, we see that:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}x^{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\left[x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]\right]_{x=0}\\
&\quad\quad=\sum_{\{n_{k}\geq 0\}_{k}}\prod_{k=1}^{p}\frac{(N^{1-k}\alpha_{k}/k)^{n_{k}}}{n_{k}!}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell(\mathbf{n})}}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell(\mathbf{n})}}x^{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\left[x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]\right]_{x=0},\end{split}

(313)

where
\ell(\mathbf{n})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}kn_{k}
. The bracket on the right hand side of this equation is non-vanishing only if
\ell(\mathbf{n})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}kn_{k}\geq r
and
r
of the derivatives act on
x^{r}
, in which case:

\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell(\mathbf{n})}}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell(\mathbf{n})}}x^{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\left[x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]\right]_{x=0}=r!\binom{\ell(\mathbf{n})}{r}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell(\mathbf{n})-r+s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell(\mathbf{n})-r+s}}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]_{x=0}.

(314)

The right hand side of Eq. (
312
) is therefore equal to:

\sum_{\bigl\{n_{k}\geq 0\;\mid\;\ell(\mathbf{n})\geq\,r\bigr\}}\prod_{k=1}^{p}\frac{(N^{1-k}\alpha_{k}/k)^{n_{k}}}{n_{k}!}\frac{\ell(\mathbf{n})!}{(\ell(\mathbf{n})-r)!}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell(\mathbf{n})-r+s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell(\mathbf{n})-r+s}}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]_{x=0}.

(315)

We now focus on the left-hand side of Eq. (
312
):

\displaystyle\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}}y^{s}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\geq 0}\frac{1}{q!}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{q}}{\mathrm{d}y^{q}}y^{s}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}\right]\frac{\mathrm{d}^{q}}{\mathrm{d}x^{q}}

(316)

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\geq s}\frac{s!}{q!}\binom{q}{s}\hskip-2.84544pt\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{q+r-s}}{\mathrm{d}y^{q+r-s}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}\right]\frac{\mathrm{d}^{q}}{\mathrm{d}x^{q}},

(317)

in which the three brackets are evaluated in
y=0
. We develop:

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{q+r-s}}{\mathrm{d}y^{q+r-s}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}\right]_{y=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{n_{k}\geq 0\}_{k}}\prod_{k=1}^{p}\frac{(N^{1-k}\alpha_{k}/k)^{n_{k}}}{n_{k}!}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{q+r-s}}{\mathrm{d}y^{q+r-s}}y^{\ell(\mathbf{n})}\right]_{y=0}

(318)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\{n_{k}\geq 0\}\textrm{ s.t.}}\\[1.50694pt]
{\ell(\mathbf{n})=q+r-s}\end{subarray}}\prod_{k=1}^{p}\frac{(N^{1-k}\alpha_{k}/k)^{n_{k}}}{n_{k}!}\ell(\mathbf{n})!.

(319)

Replacing this in Eq. (
316
) and then Eq. (
316
) in the left-hand side of Eq. (
312
) leads to the same expansion Eq. (
315
).
We have shown so far that:

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=f_{N}\det\left\{M_{i,j}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1},

(320)

where:

\displaystyle M_{(p-1)r+s\;,\;j}

\displaystyle=({N^{p-1}}/{\alpha_{p}})^{r}\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}x^{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\Bigl[x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\Bigr]\right]_{x=0}

(321)

\displaystyle=({N^{p-1}}/{\alpha_{p}})^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y}{2\pi/\imath}\,\textrm{e}^{-xy}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}x^{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\Bigl[x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\Bigr].

(322)

The equivalence between Eq. (
321
) and Eq. (
322
) is the usual one-matrix differential formulation of two-matrix models of Sec.
25.2
but for
A,B
two real variables, and seeing
x^{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}[x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}]
as a function of
x
.

26.2.2
Computation in the integral formulation

Starting from Eq. (
257
), we change variable as
y^{\prime}=Ny
and include the normalization by
2\pi/\imath
:

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=f_{N}\det\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y}{2\pi/\imath}\,\textrm{e}^{-xy}x^{i}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}x)}y^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(y)}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1},

(323)

where we have used the fact that
f_{N}=(2\pi/\imath)^{N}d_{N}^{-1}N^{-\frac{N(N+1)}{2}}
.
The steps up to equation Eq. (
311
) are precisely the same in integral form, so we do not detail them again. While a perturbative proof of Eq. (
312
) is certainly possible, it does not seem to us that the proof above can directly be translated in integral form (in particular the steps Eq. (
316
) to Eq. (
319
)), so we propose a different proof. In integral form, Eq. (
311
) reads

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=f_{N}\det_{i,j}\left\{(N^{1-p}\alpha_{p})^{-r(i)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y}{2\pi/\imath}\,\textrm{e}^{-xy}y^{s(i)}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r(i)}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r(i)}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(y)}\right]x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right\},

(324)

again with
0\leq i,j<N
. By performing
r
integrations by part:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\textrm{e}^{-xy}y^{s}&\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(y)}\right]x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\\
&=(-1)^{r}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r}}\textrm{e}^{-xy}y^{s}\right]\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(y)}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)},\end{split}

(325)

where the boundary terms are assumed to vanish every time due to the exponential terms. We use the following identity:

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r}}\bigl[(ay)^{s}\textrm{e}^{axy}\bigr]=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\bigl[(ax)^{r}\textrm{e}^{axy}\bigr].

(326)

Indeed, using Leibniz formula:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r}}\left[(ay)^{s}\textrm{e}^{axy}\right]&=\sum_{k=0}^{r}\binom{r}{k}a^{s}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}y^{k}}\left[y^{s}\right]\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r-k}}{\mathrm{d}y^{r-k}}\left[\textrm{e}^{axy}\right]\\
&=\textrm{e}^{axy}\sum_{k=0}^{\min(r,s)}\frac{1}{k!}\frac{s!}{(s-k)!}\frac{r!}{(r-k)!}\frac{(ay)^{s}(ax)^{r}}{(axy)^{k}}.\end{split}

(327)

The same expression is obtained developing
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\left[(ax)^{r}\textrm{e}^{axy}\right]
. Using this identity, Eq. (
325
) is equal to:

\displaystyle\begin{split}(-1)^{s}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(y)}&\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\textrm{e}^{-xy}x^{r}\right]x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\\
&=\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\textrm{e}^{-xy}y^{s}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(y)}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right],\end{split}

(328)

where the equality is obtained by doing again
s
integrations by parts.
We thus recover Eq. (
322
).

26.3
Potential application to new orthogonal polynomial method

26.3.1
Equivalence between the diagonalized differential formulations for one-matrix models

Performing the steps of Sec.
26.1.1
, but starting from the differential formulation Eq. (
291
) of one-matrix models leads to:

Z_{V}=f_{N}\,\det\left\{\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i}}{\mathrm{d}x^{i}}\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2N}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV(x)}\right]_{x=0}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1}.

(329)

This is also the differential formulation of the Slater determinant expression Eq. (
298
) for a two-matrix model with
V_{1}(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}
.

On the other hand, performing steps similar to Sec.
24.2.4
to express Eq. (
292
) in determinant form, we obtain:

Z_{V}=N!e_{N}\,\det\left\{\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2N}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}}x^{i+j}\textrm{e}^{NV(x)}\right]_{x=0}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1}.

(330)

From the simpler formulation Eq. (
330
), one can use
orthogonal
polynomials satisfying

\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2N}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}}P_{i}(x)P_{j}(x)\textrm{e}^{NV(x)}\right]_{x=0}=\delta_{ij}p_{i}

(331)

instead of
biorthogonal
polynomials Eq. (
258
) satisfying

\left[P_{i}\Bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\Bigr)\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2N}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}}Q_{j}(x)\textrm{e}^{NV(x)}\right]_{x=0}=\delta_{ij}p_{i},

(332)

a priori
leading to a simpler resolution.

The computations of Sec.
26.2
in the differential formulation, in the case where
V_{1}(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}
(
p=2
,
\alpha_{2}=1
), prove the equivalence between Eq. (
329
) and Eq. (
330
). Indeed for
p=2
,
r(i)=i
so that the coefficients
({N^{p-1}}/{\alpha_{p}})^{r(i)}=N^{i}
in the determinant just provide an overall factor
N^{N(N-1)/2}
, and
f_{N}N^{\frac{N(N-1)}{2}}=N!e_{N}
.

In integral form, the proof is simpler, as the Gaussian integration for
V_{1}(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}
can be carried out explicitly after changing variables for
x^{\prime}=Nx/\imath
.
50
50

50

There is an important subtlety in changing variables from a real variable to a purely imaginary variable: in addition to the Jacobian of the change of variables, an additional factor
-1
has to be taken into account. This is detailed in Appendix
14
, see Eq. (
392
). The problem does not appear for the one-matrix model in the computations in differential formulation of Sec.
26.2
, or using the heat kernel Eq. (
335
).
Equivalently, one may recover directly the Slater determinant form of the partition function of one-matrix models in the integral formulation, that is

Z_{V_{2}}=\frac{N!}{b_{N}}\det\left\{\int\mathrm{d}y\,\textrm{e}^{-N\frac{y^{2}}{2}}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(y)}y^{i+j}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1},

(333)

from the differential formulation of the Slater determinant Eq. (
298
) (after the change of variables Eq. (
306
)),

Z_{{x^{2}}/2,V_{2}}=f_{N}\det\left\{\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i}}{\mathrm{d}x^{i}}\textrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2N}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}}x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\right]_{x=0}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1},

(334)

by using the
heat kernel
formulation:

\textrm{e}^{t\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}}F(x)=\int\mathrm{d}y\,K_{t}(x-y)F(y),\qquad K_{t}(x-y)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi t}}e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{4t}},

(335)

for
t=\frac{1}{2N}
and
F(x)=x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}
. We get:

Z_{{x^{2}}/2,V_{2}}=f_{N}\det\left\{\sqrt{\frac{N}{2\pi}}\int\mathrm{d}y\left[\frac{\partial^{i}}{\partial x^{i}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{N(x-y)^{2}}{2}}\right]_{x=0}y^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(y)}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1},

(336)

where the bracket evaluating
x
at 0 has been moved inside the integral, as only the derivative of the kernel depends on
x
. One can verify that

\left[\frac{\partial^{i}}{\partial x^{i}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{N(x-y)^{2}}{2}}\right]_{x=0}=N^{i}\tilde{Q}_{i}(y)\textrm{e}^{-\frac{N}{2}y^{2}},

(337)

where
\tilde{Q}_{i}(y)
is a monic polynomial of degree
i
, so we do recover Eq. (
333
) as
f_{N}(\frac{N}{2\pi})^{\frac{N}{2}}N^{\frac{N(N-1)}{2}}=N!/b_{N}
.

One can also use the heat kernel to find the integral representation of Eq. (
291
) and the one-matrix version of Eq. (
296
), again obtained by setting
V_{1}(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}
. Applying the
N
-dimensional version of Eq. (
335
) to Eq. (
291
) for each
x_{i}
we find

Z_{V}=\left(\frac{N}{2\pi}\right)^{\frac{N}{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\Delta(\mathbf{x})}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\,\textrm{e}^{-\frac{N(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})^{2}}{2}}\Delta(\mathbf{y})\,e^{NV(\mathbf{y})}\right]_{\mathbf{x}=0}.

(338)

Similarly, in the one-matrix version of Eq. (
296
) we can use the heat kernel to find

Z_{x^{2}/2,V_{2}}=\left[\frac{1}{\Delta(\mathbf{x})}\det\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int\mathrm{d}y\,\textrm{e}^{-\frac{(x_{i}-y)^{2}}{2}}y^{j-1}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}y\right)}\right\}_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\right]_{\mathbf{x}=0}.

(339)

26.3.2
Application to new orthogonal polynomial methods

As we have seen at the beginning of the present section, the computations of Sec.
26.2
in the case where
V_{1}(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}
, prove the equivalence in the differential formulation between Eq. (
329
) and Eq. (
330
), and the latter expression allows using orthogonal polynomials Eq. (
331
) instead of biorthogonal polynomials Eq. (
332
). One may naturally wonder if for potentials
V_{1}
of degree higher than two, our new expressions Eq. (
321
), Eq. (
322
) could also allow the use of orthogonal polynomials instead of biorthogonal polynomials for two-matrix models.

To this aim, we reorganize the lines of the matrix
M
in Eq. (
321
) according to their remainder modulo
p-1
, that is:

\begin{split}Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=h_{N}\det\left\{\tilde{M}_{i,j}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1},\quad\\
\mathrm{where}\quad\tilde{M}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}T_{0}\\
\hline\cr\vdots\\
\hline\cr T_{p-1}\end{array}\right)\quad\mathrm{and}\quad(T_{s})_{r,j}=M_{(p-1)r+s\;,\;j}.\end{split}

(340)

where
h_{N}=f_{N}\,\mathrm{sgn}(N,p)
,
\mathrm{sgn}(N,p)
being the parity of the permutation of rows, and we recall (Eq. (
321
)) that in differential formulation,

M_{(p-1)r+s\;,\;j}=({N^{p-1}}/{\alpha_{p}})^{r}\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}x^{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\Bigl[x^{j}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\Bigr]\right]_{x=0}.

(341)

If
s_{0}
and
r_{0}
are respectively the remainder and the quotient of the Euclidean division of
N-1
by
p-1
, then for
0\leq s\leq s_{0}
, the matrix
T_{s}
has
r_{0}+1
lines, while for
s_{0}<s<p-1
,
T_{s}
has
r_{0}
lines.

By reorganizing the columns of
\tilde{M}
, and the lines of
T_{s}
independently for each
s
, we may replace
({N^{p-1}}/{\alpha_{p}})^{-r}(T_{s})_{r,j}=({N^{p-1}}/{\alpha_{p}})^{-r}M_{(p-1)r+s\;,\;j}
in the determinant by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}P_{r}^{(s)}(x)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\Bigl[Q_{j}(x)\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\Bigr]\right]_{x=0}\\
=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y}{2\pi/\imath}\,\textrm{e}^{-xy}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}P_{r}^{(s)}(x)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{s}}{\mathrm{d}x^{s}}\Bigl[Q_{j}(x)\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\Bigr],\end{split}

(342)

where for
0\leq s<p-1
, the
P_{r}^{(s)}(x)
are monic polynomials of degree
r
, and
Q_{j}
is a monic polynomial of degree
j
.

One may for instance choose
P_{r}^{(s)}=Q_{r}
for all
0\leq s<p-1
. We use the following notation for the determinant obtained this way:

Z_{V_{1},V_{2}}=h_{N}\det\left\{W_{i,j}\right\}_{0\leq i,j\leq N-1},\quad\mathrm{where}\quad W=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{0}\\
\hline\cr\vdots\\
\hline\cr J_{p-1}\end{array}\right).

(343)

For
s=0
, the elements
(J_{0})_{r,j}
of the resulting matrix
J_{0}
define the symmetric bilinear form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle Q_{r}\mid\mid Q_{j}\rangle&=\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}Q_{r}(x)Q_{j}(x)\right]_{x=0}\\
&=\frac{\imath}{2\pi}\int\mathrm{d}x\,Q_{r}(x)Q_{j}(x)\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\int\mathrm{d}y\,\textrm{e}^{-xy}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)},\end{split}

(344)

and requiring
\langle Q_{r}\mid\mid Q_{j}\rangle=h_{r}\delta_{r,j}
defines a family of orthogonal polynomials for the following weight:

\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}\int\mathrm{d}y\,\textrm{e}^{-xy}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}.

(345)

In addition to the usual three-terms recurrence
Q_{n+1}(x)=(x-\beta_{n})Q_{n}-\frac{h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}Q_{n-1}
satisfied by any family of orthogonal polynomials, to obtain families of recurrence relations on the coefficients
\beta_{n}
and
h_{n}
, one may for instance use the fact that

\displaystyle\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}V_{1}^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\Bigr)F(x)\right]_{x=0}

\displaystyle=\left[\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}}V_{1}^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N}y\Bigr)\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}\right]_{y=0}F(x)\right]_{x=0}

(346)

\displaystyle=\left[\left[\textrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}y)}\right]_{y=0}F(x)\right]_{x=0},

(347)

so that

\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}V_{1}^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\Bigr)F(x)\right]_{x=0}=\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}xF(x)\right]_{x=0},

(348)

applied to
F(x)=Q_{r}(x)Q_{j}(x)\textrm{e}^{NV_{2}(x)}
for any
r,j
. For instance for the case
V_{1}(x)=x^{3}/3
, for which the weight for the orthogonal polynomials includes the Airy function as a factor, this equation
leads to the family of equations

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{2}{N}\langle V_{2}^{\prime}Q_{r}^{\prime}\mid\mid Q_{j}\rangle&+\frac{2}{N}\langle V_{2}^{\prime}Q_{r}\mid\mid Q_{j}^{\prime}\rangle+\langle R\,Q_{r}\mid\mid Q_{j}\rangle\\
&+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\Bigl(\langle Q_{r}^{\prime\prime}\mid\mid Q_{j}\rangle+\langle Q_{r}\mid\mid Q_{j}^{\prime\prime}\rangle+2\langle Q_{r}^{\prime}\mid\mid Q_{j}^{\prime}\rangle\Bigr)=0,\end{split}

(349)

with
R(x)=\frac{1}{N}V_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x)+(V_{2}^{\prime}(x))^{2}-x
.

The rest of the determinant Eq. (
343
) for
s>0
, obtained from Eq. (
342
), also involves sums of terms of the form
\langle g\,Q_{r}\mid\mid Q_{j}^{(k)}\rangle
for some polynomials
g(x)
. Computing the determinant therefore requires knowledge about the derivatives of the families of orthogonal polynomials with weight Eq. (
345
). It is not clear at this point whether the family of orthogonal polynomials can indeed be constructed and whether the rest of the determinant can be computed this way.

On the other hand, everything seems to simplify drastically at large
N
: for the choice
P_{r}^{(s)}=Q_{r}
for all
0\leq s<p-1
, the leading contributions in
N
to the matrix elements Eq. (
342
) for the part of the determinant corresponding to
s>0
are obtained when all
s
derivatives act on
e^{NV_{2}(x)}
, raising a factor
N^{s}(V_{2}^{\prime}(x))^{s}
, so that the elements of the determinant simplify to

(J_{s})_{r,j}\sim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{N^{(p-1)r+s}}{\alpha_{p}^{r}}\left\langle(V_{2}^{\prime})^{s}Q_{r}\mid\mid Q_{j}\right\rangle.

(350)

In the same way, the leading contribution to the family of relations Eq. (
348
) seems to be given by

\displaystyle\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}e^{NV_{2}(x)}V_{1}^{\prime}\bigl(V_{2}^{\prime}(x)\bigr)Q_{r}(x)Q_{j}(x)\right]_{x=0}

\displaystyle=\left[\textrm{e}^{NV_{1}(\frac{1}{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x})}xF(x)\right]_{x=0},

(351)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\langle UQ_{r}\mid\mid Q_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

(352)

with
U(x)=V_{1}^{\prime}\bigl(V_{2}^{\prime}(x)\bigr)-x
.
However, this already goes beyond the original scope of the chapter, and we leave this for future work.

27
Summary and conclusion

We have studied a reformulation of one- and two-matrix models in a language of differential operators. This is especially interesting in the context of two-matrix models, where an integral over two matrix variables can be recast into an expression containing only one matrix variable and its derivatives. For such two-matrix models, we explicitly showed how the Harish-Chandra–Itzykson–Zuber formula can be used to diagonalize the matrix variables in both the differential and integral settings. Furthermore, we demonstrated that the partition functions for both one- and two-matrix models can be written in terms of Slater determinants. This Slater determinant expression leads to the decomposition of the partition function into inner products of (bi-)orthogonal polynomials.

We organize the many different (but equivalent) expressions of the one-matrix model partition function in Fig.
48
, and those of the two-matrix model partition function in Fig.
49
. This highlights the overall structure of our work, and points out the relations that exist between the various partition function expressions encountered in this chapter.

The decomposition of matrix model partition functions in terms of (bi-)orthogonal polynomials can lead to exact solutions of a model’s correlation functions, provided that one can explicitly find the appropriate family (or families) of polynomials. This can, for instance, be achieved by solving recurrence relations obtained from inner products between expressions involving the polynomials. This is generally simpler for one-matrix models, where one can make use of families of orthogonal polynomials, as opposed to families of biorthogonal polynomials for the two-matrix case. Our reformulation of the two-matrix model partition function in terms of a single matrix variable and its derivatives suggests that it may also be possible to solve such models using families of orthogonal instead of biorthogonal polynomials. Finally, we propose a possible starting point for such a procedure in Sec.
26.3.2
.

Chapter 7
Quantum gravity on the computer

The CDT partition function after Wick rotation takes the form of a sum over configurations with a real Boltzmann weight dictated by the Regge action. We can therefore view CDT quantum gravity as a
statistical
theory of random geometry. As discussed in Sec.
2
of the Introduction, we can compute quantum gravitational expectation values
\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle
of observables by determining their ensemble average:

\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle=\frac{1}{Z}\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\frac{1}{C_{T}}\,\mathcal{O}[T]\,e^{-S_{E}[T]},

(353)

where the normalization factor
Z
is the partition function (
6
) of the system and
S_{E}[T]
is the CDT Regge action (
9
). However, analytically computing expectation values of observables is often intractable. In these cases, we can make use of computer simulations to sample the geometric ensemble, allowing us to compute an estimator of an observable’s quantum expectation value. The technical underpinnings of such simulations are comprehensively described in
[
25
]
. This chapter aims to provide a hands-on introduction to the basic principles involved in CDT computer simulations, and to explain in detail how these principles can be put into practice. We start by discussing the general set-up of Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods for random geometry. Next, we describe basic ingredients of CDT simulations in two and three dimensions. We subsequently explain some of the difficulties one encounters when requiring these simulations to run efficiently, and how a particular method for storing simplices in memory can be used to address these issues. Finally, we discuss the three stages of a simulation run: tuning of bare coupling constants to their pseudocritical value, evolution of the initial geometry to a well-thermalized state, and the measurement of observables on the geometry.

The latter part of this chapter is heavily based on our own implementation of CDT simulations in two and three dimensions. The associated C++ implementation code
[
74
,
119
]
has been open-sourced and is freely available for use by quantum gravity enthousiasts.

28
Monte Carlo methods for random geometry

Our aim is to generate a sample
X
of an ensemble
\mathcal{T}
of triangulations, with the following probability distribution
P(T)
for drawing a triangulation
T
from the sample:

P(T)=\frac{1}{Z}e^{-S_{E}(T)}.

(354)

By computing the sample mean of an observable
\mathcal{O}
over
X
we obtain an estimator
\widehat{\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle}
of the ensemble average
\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle
. In order to generate the sample
X
we can make use of Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods. The idea behind this approach is to construct a random walk (in the form of a Markov chain) through the space of triangulations, where we perform local updates to a geometry at each step of the walk. By enforcing certain conditions on this random walk, we can ensure that the probability of encountering a specific triangulation
T
in the random walk approaches (
354
). If we sample from this random walk with a sufficiently large number of local updates in between subsequent draws, we obtain a set of independent triangulations with the desired probability distribution. This set forms the sample
X
. It is useful to point out here that we work with
labeled
triangulations in computer simulations, and that triangulations related by a relabeling of building blocks should be counted only once. This requires us to introduce a combinatorial factor to correct for this overcounting, so that for a labeled triangulation
T
we have

P_{l}(T)=\frac{1}{Z}\frac{1}{N_{0}(T)!}e^{-S_{E}(T)},

(355)

where
N_{0}(T)
is the number of vertices present in the geometry. We label the vertices instead of the higher-dimensional simplices, since the higher-dimensional simplices can be described by lists of vertex labels.

In practice, it is convenient to start the random walk from a simple triangulation that we construct “by hand”. This is a special choice of starting point, and in order to avoid that any bias affects the measurement results one should minimize dependence on this choice of initial geometry. We therefore let the simulation run for a long time in order to let the system
thermalize
. After this thermalization phase is complete, we have obtained an independent triangulation that can be used as the initial configuration for a series of measurements. In between subsequent measurements, we perform a large number of local updates, which we group together in batches, also called
sweeps
. The size of a sweep should be taken large enough to ensure that significant autocorrelations with the previously measured geometry have disappeared. However, larger sweeps require longer simulation times, and we typically have to strike a balance between reducing autocorrelation influences and increasing execution time. Later in this chapter we discuss how to determine appropriate lengths for thermalization phases and sweeps.

We refer the interested reader to
[
73
]
for a more detailed explanation of the MCMC approach and associated practical issues like autocorrelation effects, and now proceed to briefly discuss the main elements relevant for our purposes.

28.1
Random walks and detailed balance

The first condition we should enforce on the random walk is
ergodicity
, meaning that all triangulations in the geometric ensemble
\mathcal{T}
can be reached in a finite number of steps. Without ergodicity, we would be restricted to a subspace of the ensemble, potentially making the estimators of quantum expectation values unreliable. The second condition relates to the transition probabilities of a single step in the random walk. Let us write the probability for transitioning from a triangulation
T
to a new triangulation
T^{\prime}
in a single step by
p(T\to T^{\prime})
. In order to let the probability distribution of the random walk converge to (
354
), it is sufficient to demand that the transition probabilities satisfy the condition of
detailed balance
:

P_{l}(T)p(T\to T^{\prime})=P_{l}(T^{\prime})p(T^{\prime}\to T).

(356)

For the ensembles considered in this thesis, it is convenient to restrict the transitions to a basic set of small local updates to the triangulations, and as a consequence most transition probabilities are set to zero. For the allowed transitions a solution to the detailed balance equation (
356
) is

\displaystyle\frac{P_{l}(T)}{P_{l}(T^{\prime})}=\frac{p(T^{\prime}\to T)}{p(T\to T^{\prime})}.

(357)

We furthermore allow for trivial transitions
T\to T
from a triangulation to itself, i.e.
p(T\to T)\neq 0
. Labeling every step of the walk with an integer “time” parameter
n
, the random walk consists of a sequence of triangulations
T_{n}
. The updating procedure then works as follows. At step
n
of the random walk, we have that
T_{n}=T
. We select one of the allowed local updates
T\to T^{\prime}
with a
selection probability

g(T\to T^{\prime})
. This proposed update, also called a
move
, is subsequently accepted with probability
A(T\to T^{\prime})
, and rejected otherwise. The probability
A(T\to T^{\prime})
for a move to be accepted is called the
acceptance ratio
. In case it is accepted, we set the next triangulation in the sequence to be
T_{n+1}=T^{\prime}
, and if the move is rejected the triangulation is unchanged and we set
T_{n+1}=T
.

The transition probability for the move
T\to T^{\prime}
can be decomposed as

p(T\to T^{\prime})=g(T\to T^{\prime})A(T\to T^{\prime}).

(358)

The selection probability
g(T\to T^{\prime})
depends on the set of basic moves and the details of the triangulation
T
at hand. Subsequently, the acceptance ratio
A(T\to T^{\prime})
should be chosen in such a way to ensure detailed balance. A simple choice is the one made in the
Metropolis-Hastings algorithm

[
120
,
163
]
, and it amounts to setting

\displaystyle A(T\to T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,\frac{g(T^{\prime}\to T)P_{l}(T^{\prime})}{g(T\to T^{\prime})P_{l}(T)}\right),

(359)

\displaystyle A(T^{\prime}\to T)

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,\frac{g(T\to T^{\prime})P_{l}(T)}{g(T^{\prime}\to T)P_{l}(T^{\prime})}\right).

(360)

It is instructive to see how this works in the two example scenarios of two- and three-dimensional CDT, and how the choice of the move implementation affects the selection probabilities and associated acceptance ratios. In what follows, we denote by an
(n,m)
-move a geometric update on a neighborhood consisting of a number
n
of
d
-simplices before the move and a number
m
of
d
-simplices after the move.

28.2
Metropolis algorithm for 2D CDT

We start by computing the probability
P_{l}(T)
of encountering a certain labeled triangulation
T
in the ensemble of 2D CDT with toroidal topology. Recalling the Regge action (
11
) for CDT in two dimensions and substituting this into Eq. (
355
), we find

P_{l}(T)=\frac{1}{Z}\frac{1}{N_{0}(T)!}e^{-\lambda N_{2}(T)}.

(361)

This expression will be used to compute the left-hand side of Eq. (
357
) for a certain move and its inverse. Only two simple moves (and their inverses) suffice to construct an ergodic random walk through the space of two-dimensional CDT geometries with a fixed number of time slices and an either even or odd number of triangles. We illustrate the moves in Fig.
50
, where we show spacelike links in blue and timelike links in red. Furthermore, time labels of the slices increase in the upwards direction, so that a 21-simplex is a triangle pointing up and a 12-simplex is a triangle pointing down.
51
51

51

Here we follow the shorthand simplex type naming convention introduced in Sec.
2
, so that a
pq
-simplex contains
p
vertices in the slice labeled
t
and
q
vertices in the slice labeled
(t+1)
.
The first move, denoted the
(2,4)
-move, takes two adjacent triangles sharing a spacelike link, and replaces it by four triangles. We also call this the
add
move. Its inverse, the
(4,2)
-move or
delete
move, takes four triangles sharing a single vertex of order four (the order of a vertex is defined as the number of triangles that contain this vertex), and replaces them by two triangles. The second move, called the
(2,2)
-move or
flip
move, takes two adjacent triangles sharing a timelike link, and flips this link between them. This move is only allowed when one of the two triangles is of 12-type and the other is of 21-type, otherwise we would introduce timelike links connecting vertices on the same time slice. The
(2,2)
-move is its own inverse.

Figure 50
:
The two basic move sets used in 2D CDT:
add
/
delete
(top) and
flip
(bottom). The simplices consist of spacelike (blue) and timelike (red) links. The labels of the vertices are for identification purposes.

28.2.1
The
add
and
delete
moves

We start by considering the
add
move and its inverse, the
delete
move. Performing the
add
move on a triangulation
T
consisting of
N_{2}
triangles results in a triangulation
T^{\prime}
consisting of
(N_{2}+2)
triangles. A simple method for implementing the
add
move given a starting triangulation
T
is as follows. We randomly select a single triangle
t
in the triangulation, with uniform probability
1/N_{2}
per triangle (where
N_{2}
is the total number of triangles in the system). We then find the unique triangle
tv
that shares a timelike link with
t
, i.e. its
v
ertical neighbor. These triangles can also be described by their constituent vertices as marked in Fig.
50
, so that we have
t

=(012)
and
tv

=(031)
(vertices are listed in counterclockwise order by convention). Note that
t
is of type 21 in this case, and the move should be understood to proceed analogously if the input triangle
t
is of type 12.

The proposed move is to insert a new vertex, labeled 4 in the Figure, and two triangles
tn

=(412)
and
tvn

=(431)
on the right of
t
and
tv
, respectively. The original triangles get their vertices reassigned as
t

=(042)
and
tv

=(034)
. We denote the resulting triangulation
T^{\prime}
, with
(N_{2}+2)
triangles. Note that as a part of this update, one vertex and three links were added to the geometry. The new vertex requires a label in the full triangulation (these labels are generally different from the ones shown in the Figure), and we can choose to insert it anywhere in the “list” of existing vertices. This implies that there are
(N_{0}+1)
ways of assigning this label, while arriving at the same abstract triangulation
T^{\prime}
. Furthermore, the resulting triangulation is the same regardless of whether we selected
t
or its vertical neighbor
tv
initially, so we should multiply the probability of going from
T
to
T^{\prime}
by two. The overall
selection probability
for this move is therefore
g(T\to T^{\prime})=\frac{2}{N_{2}\cdot(N_{0}+1)}=\frac{1}{N_{0}\cdot(N_{0}+1)}
, where we used that
N_{2}=2N_{0}
for 2D CDT with toroidal topology.

We do not need to perform any checks before proposing this move, since it can be carried out for any input triangle
t
in the geometry. This is not the case for its inverse, the (4,2)-move, since it requires that the vertex shared by the four initial triangles is of order four. We now present two distinct approaches for implementing the
delete
move, where we will see that this influences how we should choose the acceptance ratios. Since we used an initial triangulation
T
on which we perform the
add
move, we take the resulting triangulation
T^{\prime}
as a starting point for the
delete
move in order to compute the terms in Eq. (
360
).

Method I: blind guessing.

The simplest approach amounts to randomly selecting a vertex
v
in the triangulation
T^{\prime}
with uniform probability
\frac{1}{N_{0}(T^{\prime})}=\frac{1}{N_{0}(T)+1}
. If
v
is not of order four, do not perform any update to the geometry, and let the next triangulation in the random walk again be
T^{\prime}
. On the other hand, if
v
is of order four, find the four triangles that contain
v
=
(4)
, as shown in Fig.
50
. Now delete the triangles
(412),(431)
from the system, and reassign the vertices of the other two as
(042)\to(012),(034)\to(031)
. This move only brings us back to the triangulation
T
that we started out with if the selected vertex
v
is the one that was added to the system by the
add
move discussed earlier, so that the selection probability for the move
T^{\prime}\to T
equals
g(T^{\prime}\to T)=\frac{1}{N_{0}(T)+1}
. Plugging this into Eq. (
360
), we find the acceptance ratios

\displaystyle A(T\to T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,\frac{N_{0}(T)}{N_{0}(T)+1}e^{-2\lambda}\right),

(362)

\displaystyle A(T^{\prime}\to T)

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,\frac{N_{0}(T)+1}{N_{0}(T)}e^{2\lambda}\right).

(363)

The exponential terms are due to the change in the action, and these would appear regardless of the choice of selection process. On the other hand, the ratio
N_{0}/(N_{0}+1)
arises on combinatorial grounds, and is tied to the specific implementation of the moves.

Method II: bookkeeping.

The previous method is simple to implement, but one may expect that it will often result in no change to the triangulation. After all, the move is aborted if the selected vertex is not of order four. In a thermalized configuration, approximately
1/4
of the vertices are of order four
[
55
]
, so that on average the configuration is updated at most a quarter of the times that a
delete
move is proposed. We may expect that we can do better by keeping track of the order-four vertices throughout the simulation, and to select these sites as input for the
delete
move. The move is then always legal, and it remains to compute the selection probabilities and the appropriate acceptance ratios. The selection probability for the
add
move is unchanged, but the selection probability for the
delete
move now reads
g(T^{\prime}\to T)=\frac{1}{N_{\textit{vf}}\,(T)+1}
, where
N_{\textit{vf}}\,(T)
is the number of vertices of order four in the original triangulation
T
. Computing the corresponding acceptance ratios, we find

\displaystyle A(T\to T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,\frac{N_{0}(T)}{N_{\textit{vf}}\,(T)+1}e^{-2\lambda}\right),

(364)

\displaystyle A(T^{\prime}\to T)

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,\frac{N_{\textit{vf}}\,(T)+1}{N_{0}(T)}e^{2\lambda}\right).

(365)

Since
N_{\textit{vf}}\leq N_{0}
by definition, we see that the acceptance ratio for the
delete
move has in fact gone down, while it has gone up for the
add
move. However, we should take into account that the
delete
move using method I was rejected in case the selected vertex was not of order four. In two-dimensional CDT, the critical value of the cosmological constant is
\lambda_{c}=\ln 2
, and we typically perform simulations at this fixed value for
\lambda
. For thermalized configurations, the probability for a random vertex in the configuration to have order four is equal to
1/4
, so that
N_{0}\approx 4N_{\textit{vf}}
. If the system is large enough, we therefore conclude that the transition probabilities for the move
T^{\prime}\to T
are approximately the same for methods I and II. The acceptance ratio for the
add
move in method I is close to 1, and the fact that the acceptance ratios are bounded from above by 1 implies that
A(T\to T^{\prime})=1
almost always in method II.

We see that in this model, the “blind” and “bookkeeping” methods are likely to be equally useful in the case of thermalized configurations. However, it is reasonable to expect that method II is more efficient in the thermalization phase, where order-four vertices are possibly rare. Furthermore, the arguments that showed that transition rates are approximately equal hinged upon the fact that the fraction of order-four vertices is approximately constant in this model. Similar relations are not necessarily present in higher-dimensional models, and it may be the case that bookkeeping-type approaches are more efficient for certain ranges of the coupling parameters. We end the discussion on the two-dimensional model by pointing out that attaining high acceptance rates for the moves does not guarantee optimal execution times in practice. The reason is that a simple check like the one occurring in method I is computationally cheap, and it may be the case that the overhead incurred by bookkeeping-type methods slows down the simulation to an extent that it offsets the potential benefits. In many situations, it may therefore
a priori
be unclear which of the approaches provides the best performance, and one will need to carry out profiling runs in order to determine the optimal strategy for the purpose at hand.

28.2.2
The
flip
move

We now discuss the implementation of the
(2,2)
-move, also called the
flip
move. Again, we can opt for blind- or bookkeeping-type approaches. We restrict our attention to a specific method of the second kind here, since this is the approach used in the codebase
[
74
]
that we will discuss later in this Chapter. The
flip
move is its own inverse, so we do not need to distinguish two directions of the move like for
add
and
delete
.

Instead of keeping track of all vertices of order four, we now maintain a list of all triangles that have a neighbor of opposite type on its
right
. By “type” we here mean the orientation of the triangles with respect to the time ordering, i.e. a 12-type or 21-type simplex. The timelike link shared by two such triangles is a candidate for being flipped without destroying the causal structure present in the geometry. Since no triangles are created or destroyed in the
flip
move overall, we see that both the action and the combinatorial factor in Eq. (
355
) are left unchanged in this process, so that the left-hand side of Eq. (
357
) equals 1. Given a triangulation
T
, suppose there are
N_{\textit{tf}}
triangles with triangles of opposite type on their right. We select one such triangle
tl

=(032)
with uniform probability
1/N_{\textit{tf}}
, together with its right neighboring triangle
tr

=(013)
(although the triangle
tl
is of type 12 in this case, the extension to the type-21 case should be obvious). We then propose to flip the timelike link
(03)
shared by
tl
and
tr
as shown in Fig.
50
, resulting in a new triangulation
T^{\prime}
where
(03)
is replaced by the link
(12)
. The selection probability for a move taking
T
to a certain
T^{\prime}
is then equal to
g(T\to T^{\prime})=1/N_{\textit{tf}}
. Now we should pay attention to the fact that a
flip
move may change
N_{\textit{tf}}
. If the triangle
tll
on the left of
tl
was initially of the same type as
tl
, it is of opposite type after the move and
N_{\textit{tf}}
increases by 1. Equivalently,
N_{\textit{tf}}
decreases by 1 if
tll
and
tll
were initially of opposite type. By subsequently checking the type of the right neighbor
trr
of
tr
in a similar manner we can compute the updated value
N_{\textit{tf}}^{\prime}
of the triangulation
T^{\prime}
. The probability of moving back from
T^{\prime}
to
T
with a subsequent
flip
move is then equal to
g(T^{\prime}\to T)=1/N_{\textit{tf}}^{\prime}
.

As a result, we find the following acceptance ratio for the
flip
move:

\displaystyle A(T\to T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,\frac{N_{\textit{tf}}^{\prime}}{N_{\textit{tf}}}\right),

(366)

and its reciprocal for the inverse move
T^{\prime}\to T
.

28.3
Metropolis algorithm for 3D CDT

The probability for encountering a certain labeled triangulation
T
in the 3D CDT ensemble is given by

P_{l}(T)=\frac{1}{Z}\frac{1}{N_{0}(T)!}e^{k_{0}N_{0}(T)-k_{3}N_{3}(T)},

(367)

where we see the appearance of the Regge action (
12
) for this model in the exponential. There are now three classes of moves, which we describe in more detail below when computing the appropriate acceptance ratios.

The
(2,6)
- and
(6,2)
-moves — forming the first class of moves — are each other’s inverse, and we also refer to them as the
add
and
delete
moves, since they are analogous to their 2D counterparts. We show a schematic representation of the
add
and
delete
moves in Fig.
51
. The second class consists of the
(4,4)
-move, which is its own inverse. This move flips a spatial link in the system, and we also refer to it as the
flip
move, shown in Fig.
52
. The third class of moves is formed by the
(2,3)
- and
(3,2)
-moves, and we refer to these as the
shift
and
inverse shift
(or
ishift
) moves, respectively. These moves add (delete) a 22-simplex in a region consisting of one (two) 22-simplex paired with either a 31- or a 13-simplex, as presented in Fig.
53
.

Figure 51
:
The
add
and
delete
moves in 3D CDT.

Figure 52
:
The
flip
move in 3D CDT.

Figure 53
:
The
shift
and
ishift
moves in 3D CDT.

While developing the codebase
[
119
]
for simulating 3D CDT, the choice was made to implement all moves using blind-type approaches for simplicity. It is possible to include bookkeeping-type methods, in which one maintains lists of special simplices where a specific move can be performed. However, the checks that need to be performed in order to keep these lists updated are nontrivial, and one needs to make sure that the lists are complete in order to fulfill the detailed balance condition. It is difficult to implement such checks properly in a computationally efficient way, and we therefore opted to make use of blind-type approaches for the time being. However, it may be the case that appropriate bookkeeping-type methods deliver substantial improvements to the execution time in some regions of the phase space, and a further investigation of this strategy is an important item on the development roadmap of the codebase.

28.3.1
The
add
and
delete
moves

We implement the
add
move by randomly selecting a 31-simplex
t
from a triangulation
T
with uniform probability
1/N_{31}
. We denote its adjacent 13-simplex
tv
. If the move is carried out, a vertex is then added in the center of the shared spatial triangle of these two simplices, which effectively adds two 31-simplices and two 13-simplices to the system. The order of this newly created vertex is six, in the sense that the number of tetrahedra containing
v
equals six. We also note that this move adds two triangles to the
spatial
slice at the base of
t
. We refer to Fig.
51
for a visual representation of this move. The resulting triangulation
T^{\prime}
contains
(N_{0}(T)+1)
vertices and
(N_{3}(T)+4)
tetrahedra, and as a result we have that

\frac{P_{l}(T^{\prime})}{P_{l}(T)}=\frac{1}{N_{0}(T)+1}e^{k_{0}-4k_{3}}.

(368)

The selection probability for the
add
move reads
g(T\to T^{\prime})=\frac{1}{N_{31}\cdot(N_{0}+1)}
, where the factor
1/(N_{0}+1)
again arises because we can choose what label to assign to the new vertex.

For the
delete
move we pick a random vertex
v
with uniform probability
1/(N_{0}(T)+1)
from the triangulation
T^{\prime}
. Vertices of order six can only exist in the center (labeled 5) of a configuration like the one shown in Fig.
51
, and any such vertex is therefore a candidate for removal with a
delete
move. Like in the two-dimensional implementation, we therefore abort the move if this vertex is not of order six. If
v
is indeed of order six, the proposed move is to delete four of the six surrounding tetrahedra as shown in Fig.
51
. The probability that this brings us back to the triangulation
T
is therefore
g(T^{\prime}\to T)=1/(N_{0}(T)+1)
, since this only occurs when the selected vertex is the one that was added when performing the previously discussed
add
move.

Using the above results, we can compute the appropriate acceptance ratios for the
add
and
delete
moves:

\displaystyle A(T\to T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,\frac{N_{31}(T)}{N_{0}(T)+1}e^{k_{0}-4k_{3}}\right),

(369)

\displaystyle A(T^{\prime}\to T)

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,\frac{N_{0}(T)+1}{N_{31}(T)}e^{-k_{0}+4k_{3}}\right).

(370)

A major difference with the two-dimensional case is that the couplings
k_{0}
and
k_{3}
are not fixed to a specific critical value. As discussed in Sec.
2.5
,
k_{0}
can be set to some arbitrary value, and
k_{3}
is subsequently tuned to the corresponding (pseudo-)critical value
\tilde{k}_{3}(k_{0})
in order to approach the limit of infinite building blocks. The acceptance ratios are affected by this choice of
k_{0}
, resulting in a different distribution on the triangulations in the ensemble. This can have major effects on ensemble averages of observables, as we have seen repeatedly in Chapter
5
of this thesis.

It is useful to point out here that certain moves in 3D CDT, including the
delete
move, can introduce degeneracies in the triangulation. For example, it is possible that two of the 31-simplices have a common neighbor tetrahedron
tn
. When the three 31-simplices are “merged” into one 31-simplex
t
, the simplex
tn
neighbors
t
on two of its faces. In the resulting geometry, there are two distinct spatial links connecting the same two vertices, which violates the simplicial manifold conditions. We typically exclude such configurations from consideration, and certain checks should be put into place in order to avoid these degeneracies. It is possible to consider extended ensembles where degenerate triangulations are allowed, but this may affect the behavior of the system in the continuum limit. Such degenerate 3D CDT ensembles were investigated in
[
6
]
. In this thesis, we have only considered ensembles of proper simplicial manifolds, so all moves that potentially introduce degeneracies in the geometry are subject to the aforementioned checks. If a proposed move fails any of those checks, the move is rejected.

28.4
The
flip
move

As an input for the
flip
move, we pick a 31-simplex
t
from the triangulation
T
with uniform probability
1/N_{31}
. We subsequently select with uniform probability
1/3
one of its three neighboring tetrahedra
in the same slab geometry
, i.e. we do not consider the 13-simplex that shares a spatial triangle with
t
. We denote the selected tetrahedron by
tn
. If
tn
is not of type 31 (this implies it is a 22-simplex), we abort the move. Conversely, if
tn
is a 31-simplex, we flip the spatial link shared by
t
and
tn
. This also induces a flip in the spatial link shared by the adjacent 13-simplices, which we see in Fig.
52
. We again call the resulting triangulation
T^{\prime}
. Note that the
flip
move induces a link flip in the two-dimensional spatial geometry at the base of
t
.

There is no change in the number of simplices during this move, so
P_{l}(T)=P_{l}(T^{\prime})
. Furthermore, the selection probabilities for a
flip
move and its inverse are the same using this strategy of blindly selecting a site where the flip is performed. We therefore find for the acceptance ratios that
A(T\to T^{\prime})=A(T^{\prime}\to T)=1
, and the move is always accepted if the aforementioned conditions are satisfied — except, that is, if the selected flip move would introduce a degeneracy like the ones mentioned above, in which case the move is rejected.

28.5
The
shift
and
ishift
moves

The input for the
shift
move is either a 31-simplex or a 13-simplex. Both cases should be implemented in order to guarantee ergodicity, but their description is analogous so we restrict our attention to the case of a 31-simplex without loss of generality. We select this 31-simplex
t
from the triangulation
T
with uniform probability
1/N_{31}
. Subsequently we select with uniform probability
1/3
one of its three neighboring tetrahedra in the same slab geometry. Denote this neighboring tetrahedron
ta
. If
ta
is not of type 22 (i.e. it is of type 31), abort the move. If, on the other hand,
tn
is a 22-simplex, we propose the following change to the geometry. Using the labels from the top left configuration in Fig.
53
, we can denote the 31-simplex
t

=(1234)
and the 22-simplex
ta

=(2345)
. These two simplices get replaced by a 31-simplex
tn0

=(1235)
and two 22-simplices
tn1

=(1245)
and
tn2

=(1345)
. This adds a 22-simplex and a timelike link to the system, as shown in Fig.
53
.

No vertices are added during a
shift
move, so the combinatorial factor in Eq. (
355
) is unaffected. However, the addition of a 22-simplex means that
N_{3}(T^{\prime})=N_{3}(T)+1
, so the action changes in this transition. The selection probability for this move is
g(T\to T^{\prime})=\frac{1}{3N_{31}(T)}
.

The inverse move, which we call
ishift
, also takes as input a uniformly selected 31-simplex
t
, now from the triangulation
T^{\prime}
. We then pick two of its neighbors, denoted
tn0
and
tn1
. The order in which they are picked is irrelevant, so this choice can be made in three distinct ways. The move is aborted unless
tn0
and
tn1
are neighbors, and both of type 22. Otherwise, one of the 22-simplices is removed from the system, in a manner inverse to the update made during the
shift
move. We see that the selection probability for this move is again
g(T^{\prime}\to T)=\frac{1}{3N_{31}(T)}
. Taking into account the change in the action we then find from Eq. (
360
) that

\displaystyle A(T\to T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,e^{-k_{3}}\right),

(371)

\displaystyle A(T^{\prime}\to T)

\displaystyle=\textrm{min}\left(1,e^{k_{3}}\right).

(372)

Note that the geometry of the two-dimensional spatial slices is not affected by the
shift
and
ishift
moves, and they rather change the connectivity between the slices. However, this has an effect on the entropy of the slice geometries, a fact that turned out to be important in Chapter
5
of this thesis.

28.6
Volume fixing

It is often the case that we want to collect measurements at a certain fixed target volume
\tilde{N}
, especially in the context of a finite-size scaling analysis. However, there is no volume-preserving set of moves that is
ergodic
in the space of CDT geometries of fixed size, so we must allow the volume to fluctuate around
\tilde{N}
. It is difficult to tune the cosmological coupling parameter to its pseudocritical value associated with a certain target volume, and even then the fluctuations around the target volume are large, so that we only rarely encounter systems of the right size. A standard solution is to include a
volume-fixing
term
S_{\textit{fix}}
in the action, which penalizes configurations that stray too far from the target volume. A useful choice is a quadratic volume-fixing term of the form

S_{\textit{fix}}=\epsilon\left(N-\tilde{N}\right)^{2},

(373)

where
N
is the current system size and
\epsilon
sets the strength of the fixing. When this term is taken into account when computing acceptance ratios and the coupling is tuned to the pseudocritical value, the volume tends to fluctuate around the target
\tilde{N}
with a typical fluctuation size determined by
\epsilon
(with larger
\epsilon
leading to smaller fluctuations, and vice versa).

29
Implementation

In the first part of this chapter we have shown how to implement Monte Carlo simulations of CDT in principle, with the detailed balance equations fully worked out for several choices of move implementations for the two- and three-dimensional versions of the model. However, several technical issues arise when putting these ideas into practice. In what follows, we discuss some of the challenges one encounters in this process. We furthermore explain the strategies that were used to address these challenges while implementing the simulation codebases
[
74
,
119
]
. We do not aim to provide a full description of the C++ code itself, but rather focus on the generalities of our approach.

The central classes from the user’s point of view are
Universe
and
Simulation
. The class
Universe
represents the current state of the triangulation, and stores properties of the geometry in a convenient manner. It furthermore provides methods that carry out changes on the geometry. The
Simulation
class is responsible for all procedures related to the actual Monte Carlo simulation. It proposes moves and computes the detailed balance conditions, and if it decides a move should be accepted, it calls on the
Universe
to carry out the move at a given location. It furthermore triggers the measurement of observables when the time is right. The user can define custom observables as children (in the sense of object-oriented programming) of the standard parent class
Observable
. This class provides some of the basic functionality that one may require when implementing certain observables, like a breadth-first search algorithm for measuring distances or constructing metric spheres on the (dual) lattice.

Two classes that are mostly important “under the hood” are the
Pool
and
Bag
. These perform the memory management for all the simplices present in the triangulation, and the user can generally consider these as black boxes. It is useful, however, to understand the capabilities and limitations of these structures, so we explain them in further detail below.

We now briefly summarize what follows in the rest of this chapter. The first topic we discuss is that of storing and accessing a triangulation and its constituents in the computer memory. The approach we use is largely independent of the dimension of the triangulation. Dimension-specific considerations are required, however, when implementing the Monte Carlo moves that generate a random walk through the space of triangulations. We discuss these considerations in the second part of this section, both for 2D and 3D CDT. In the third part, we go into detail on the distinct phases of the simulation process: tuning of the coupling constants, thermalizing the system, and collection of measurements. We finally discuss the inclusion of observables in the fourth part, where we also briefly mention the breadth-first search (BFS) algorithm used for measuring distances on the lattice.

29.1
Storage and access

Our goal is to store a
d
-dimensional triangulation
T
in computer memory. The triangulation consists of a collection of
d
-simplices
\sigma^{(d)}\in T
, in which every such
\sigma^{(d)}
has
(d+1)
neighboring
d
-simplices.
52
52

52

Note that we only consider triangulations without boundary here.
We can also decompose the triangulation as a collection of
n
-simplices, where
n=0,1,\cdots,d
. An
n
-simplex can be described as a list of
(n+1)
vertices, or 0-simplices. In a proper simplicial manifold, an
n
-simplex is unique in the sense that no two distinct simplices consisting of the same vertices can exist.

Consider a triangulation
T
containing
N
vertices. A straightforward choice is to assign all vertices
v\in T
a distinct label from the set
\left\{0,1,\cdots,N-1\right\}
. The
n
-simplices
\sigma^{(n)}\in T
can then be denoted by lists of the vertex labels comprising them. As an example, we take the simplest possible triangulation of a 2-sphere: the tetrahedron. This is a two-dimensional triangulation containing four vertices with labels
\{0,1,2,3\}
. It consists of four 2-simplices, or triangles, each with the other three triangles as its neighbors. The triangulation has six 1-simplices, or links. We show this triangulation and the lists of its 0-, 1-, and 2-simplices in Table
2
.

vertices

{(0),(1),(2),(3)}

links

{(01), (02), (03), (12), (13), (23)}

triangles

{(012), (013), (023), (123)}

Table 2
:
The surface of a tetrahedron, seen as the minimal triangulation of a 2-sphere.

In principle, the list of triangles is sufficient to reconstruct the entire triangulation. For example, if we want to find the neighbors of a given triangle
t
, we search for all triangles that have two vertices in common with
t
. We can also make changes to the triangulation by updating the list of triangles, and potentially adding new vertex labels. For example, we can split the triangle
(013)
in three by adding a new vertex, labeled 4, in its center. We subsequently remove
(013)
from the list of triangles, and add three new triangles
(014),(034),(134)
to it.

While this strategy is sound in principle, it is not hard to see that it is inefficient in practice. Finding the neighbors of a triangle
t
requires us to search through the list of triangles until we have found three objects that match the criterion of having two vertices in common with
t
, and the average time duration of such a search scales linearly with the number of triangles present in the system. In the language of complexity theory, we say that this operation runs in
O(N_{2})
time, where
N_{2}
is the number of triangles in
T
. Our aim is to construct large triangulations in order to approach a continuum limit, and to perform enormous numbers of subsequent local updates on these geometries. When performing a single local update, we therefore clearly want to avoid operations that scale with some (positive) power of the system size whenever possible. This can be elevated to a general guiding principle for the simulation code:

Operations required for performing Monte Carlo moves on the geometry should run in
O(1)
time.

Here we again understand the big-O notation as it is used in complexity theory: we say that an operation on a
d
-dimensional CDT geometry runs in
O(1)
time — also referred to as
constant time
— if its execution time does not depend on the total number of
d
-simplices in the system. The requirement of constant execution time per basic operation precludes the use of searches through the full lists of simplices. One may expect that a straightforward object-oriented approach can solve this issue. For example, in the two-dimensional case we could formulate three classes of simplex objects:
Vertex
,
Link
, and
Triangle
. A
Triangle
object could then store lists with references to the three
Vertex
objects and the three
Link
objects that comprise it, and a list of references to the three
Triangle
objects adjacent to it. This is a step in the right direction, but not yet a solution to all our problems. The reason is that the analogous lists of references stored by
Vertex
and
Link
objects are not all of constant size. A single vertex can be a member of an arbitrary number of triangles, so that the list of triangles that contain a certain vertex is unbounded in length. One situation in which this is problematic is, for example, when executing a
delete
move in 2D CDT. Two triangles are removed from the system, and three vertices (the ones labeled
1,2,3
in the top half of Fig.
50
) should be updated to reflect this change. This again requires a search through the list of references to
Triangle
objects stored in the
Vertex
object, slowing down the procedure.
53
53

53

An additional motivation for avoiding the use of variable-length lists in the simplex objects is that they can incur an overhead in allocation time compared to fixed-length lists. However, advances in computer architecture and compiler design have brought down this overhead significantly, and it is likely that this is only a minor point of concern.

The upshot is that we should find a strategy for storing a certain minimal amount of information required to perform all desired operations on lists of simplex objects in
O(1)
time. It is convenient to maintain separate lists of simplices for each simplex dimensionality, and in what follows we consider a simplex to be of a fixed (but arbitrary) dimension
d
.

There are four basic types of operations that are required by Monte Carlo simulations of CDT:

1.

Object creation:
add a
d
-simplex to a triangulation and assign it a unique label, thereby increasing the total list size
N_{d}
.

2.

Object deletion:
remove a
d
-simplex given its label from a triangulation, thereby decreasing the total list size
N_{d}
.

3.

Object lookup:
find a
d
-simplex in memory given its label and access its properties.

4.

Random object selection:
pick a random
d
-simplex (potentially satisfying certain conditions) in the triangulation with uniform probability.

It is precisely the combination of these four operations that makes the implementation nontrivial, and we will see why this is the case in due course. We now proceed to explain the
Pool
structure we used to implement operation types 1-3 in our codebase, and treat the
Bag
structure that implements operation type 4 separately afterwards.

29.1.1
The
Pool
structure

It is clear that the deletion of an arbitrary object in a continuous list leaves behind a hole. It is possible to fill this hole by moving the last element
obj
of the list to the newly vacant position, but this introduces a new problem: all other objects that refer to
obj
should be updated to reflect the change of its position in the list, and this updating operation is potentially of
O(N)
complexity. An alternate solution is to include hole formation in our strategy, and to work with a list of fixed length. This fixed length imposes an upper bound on the number of simplices that can be created, but memory capacity is typically not the bottleneck for CDT simulations. A computing node with 4GB of memory can easily store on the order of millions of simplices, and at these system sizes long thermalization times are often the primary issue.

The
Pool
data structure implements the idea of a list with holes. A
Pool
is instantiated with a user-defined capacity
C
, corresponding to the upper bound for the number of objects that can be stored in it. At the time of instantiation, the
Pool
allocates
C
objects in memory and collects the pointers to these objects in the list
Pool::elements
. These objects all persist in memory throughout the simulation, and are only deallocated at the end of the simulation run. However, from the point of view of the simulation, these objects can be either “alive” or “dead” at distinct times, even though they remain present in memory. It is the
Pool
structure that tracks which of these objects are alive or dead. Initially, all objects are marked dead. Objects in the
Pool
are identified by an integer label that can be both nonnegative and negative. A nonnegative label means that the object is alive; a negative label means that the object is dead. The user can furthermore define custom member variables for objects in the
Pool
.

The
Pool
uses a stack-like structure to keep track of the indices of dead objects in the list
Pool::elements
. These sites in the list are candidates for objects that can be switched to the alive status. Before any objects have been made alive, the stack contains all integers in the range
[0,C-1]
, with the value 0 at the top of the stack and incrementing by 1 with each step downwards.

In order to create an object from the point of view of the simulation, we call the method
Pool::create()
. This pops the first value from the stack, i.e. the index of an object in
Pool::elements
that was dead but is now switched to alive. This index
i
is also the label of this object, so that we can always retrieve an object by accessing the pointer stored at
Pool::elements[i]
. Objects created by the
Pool
can be cast to an integer, which represents its label.

Analogously, we can destroy an object with label
i
from the point of view of the simulation by calling
Pool::destroy(i)
. The object is marked dead and the label
i
is pushed onto the stack of indices of dead objects. Before destroying an object, the
Pool
checks whether the object is indeed dead — otherwise an error is thrown, since the simulation attempting to destroy a dead object is likely the signal of a bug in the code.

Accessing an arbitrary object is straightforward: the method
Pool::at(i)
returns the object with label
i
. We see that the
Pool
structure provides us with object creation, deletion, and lookup operations with
O(1)
complexity. However, the fact that the list
Pool::elements
generically contains holes makes random selection of an object in
O(1)
time impossible. For this, we need an extra data structure that tracks the position of all the alive elements in the
Pool
. This is the
Bag
class, which we discuss next.

29.1.2
The
Bag
structure

It is straightforward to pick a random object from a contiguous list: one generates a random integer
i
in the range
[0,N-1]
, where
N
is the length of the list, and retrieves the object at index
i
in the list. However, applying this to the list
Pool::elements
does not work, since the index can point to a dead object. Similarly, we cannot iterate over all alive objects (or a subset of them) present in the
Pool
. The
Bag
class is a structure that can be tied to a
Pool
in order to provide this missing functionality.

The
Bag
contains two lists of fixed length
C
, with
C
equal to the associated
Pool
capacity. The lists are named
Bag::elements
and
Bag::indices
. The list
Bag::elements
contains the labels of all elements in this
Bag
and is
contiguous
, in the sense that it does not have holes. The list
Bag::indices
contains the index of each element in the list
Bag::indices
, so that
Bag::elements[Bag::indices[i]]
returns the object with label
i
. This linked structure allows us to add and remove objects to a
Bag
in
O(1)
time: when an object is added using
Bag::add(i)
, its label
i
is entered at
Bag::elements[Bag::size - 1]
, where
Bag::size
is the current number of objects in the
Bag
. All unused slots in
Bag::elements
are marked
EMPTY
with the constant
-1
, so that a
Bag
with a nonzero number
n
